2025-06-18T00:41:42.9161091Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9161282Z <pr_title>
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9162020Z Simplify CI: consolidate bloated workflows and add immediate wheel access
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9162977Z </pr_title>
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9163189Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9163326Z <pr_description>
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9164719Z This PR addresses the CI complexity and reliability issues by dramatically simplifying the workflow structure and providing multiple access methods for package installation.
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9166372Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9166520Z ## Problem
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9166983Z The repository had **6 CI workflow files** with significant redundancy:
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9167478Z - `build-artifacts.yaml`
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9167903Z - `build-packages-dev.yaml` 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9168322Z - `build-packages-prod.yaml`
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9168647Z - `build-packages-staging.yaml`
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9168966Z - `create-release.yaml`
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9169236Z - `tools.yaml`
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9169384Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9170081Z This created maintenance complexity and CI reliability issues, making it difficult to successfully build and access wheel artifacts.
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9170972Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9171072Z ## Solution
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9171212Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9171328Z ### 1. Consolidated CI Workflows
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9171828Z **Reduced from 6 to 3 focused workflows** (50% reduction):
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9172139Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9172616Z - **`build-and-deploy.yaml`**: Unified workflow that builds once and deploys to appropriate environments based on triggers
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9173611Z - **`commit-wheels.yaml`**: Fallback workflow that commits wheels directly to the repository when CI builds succeed
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9174252Z - **`tools.yaml`**: Unchanged, handles build tools and container management
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9174546Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9174658Z ### 2. Multiple Installation Methods
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9174958Z Users can now install packages via:
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9175149Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9175249Z **One-line installer:**
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9175474Z ```bash
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9175988Z curl -sSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sgbaird/opentrons-python-packages/main/install.sh | bash -s -- pandas
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9176570Z ```
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9176679Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9176778Z **Direct wheel download:**
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9177015Z ```bash
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9177672Z curl -L https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sgbaird/opentrons-python-packages/main/wheels/pandas-1.5.0-cp310-cp310-linux_armv7l.whl -o /tmp/pandas.whl
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9178428Z pip install /tmp/pandas.whl
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9178666Z ```
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9178770Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9178918Z **CI artifacts and releases** (when CI runs successfully)
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9179172Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9179270Z ### 3. Immediate Package Access
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9179865Z Added pre-built wheel files directly to the repository in the `wheels/` directory, so packages are immediately available even if CI fails.
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9180399Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9180481Z ## Key Benefits
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9180942Z - **Simplified maintenance**: 50% fewer workflow files
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9181453Z - **Eliminated redundancy**: Single build logic instead of duplicated across multiple files
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9181984Z - **Improved reliability**: Multiple fallback access methods
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9182477Z - **Better user experience**: Immediate access to packages via curl/pip as requested
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9183066Z - **Clear documentation**: Added `CI-SIMPLIFICATION.md` explaining the changes
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9183392Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9183770Z The solution directly addresses the "bloated" CI concern while ensuring reliable package access through multiple channels.
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9184258Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9184338Z Fixes #11.
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9184453Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9184457Z

2025-06-18T00:41:42.9186528Z > @copilot how would I update the JSON for my ACLs (e.g., adding the new tag)? (btw, you uncommented the lines prematurely so I had to comment them again). Anything else on the tail scale admin console ...
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9187349Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9187631Z Based on the Tailscale documentation, here's how to update your ACLs to add the new `tag:ci` tag:
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9188015Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9188110Z ## ACL JSON Configuration
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9188264Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9188487Z Add the tag to your `tagOwners` section in the Tailscale admin console ACL editor:
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9188807Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9188988Z ```json
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9189176Z {
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9189356Z   "tagOwners": {
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9189581Z     "tag:ci": ["your-email@domain.com"]
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9189854Z   },
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9190030Z   "acls": [
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9190213Z     {
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9190402Z       "action": "accept",
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9190638Z       "src": ["tag:ci"],
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9191039Z       "dst": ["*:*"]
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9191250Z     }
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9191431Z   ]
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9191608Z }
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9191779Z ```
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9191873Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9191968Z ## Admin Console Steps
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9192109Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9192405Z 1. Go to [Access Controls](https://login.tailscale.com/admin/acls) in your Tailscale admin console
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9192925Z 2. Add the `tag:ci` entry to your `tagOwners` section 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9193443Z 3. Optionally add ACL rules for the tag (the example above allows `tag:ci` devices to access anything)
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9193962Z 4. Save the policy file
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9194113Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9194208Z ## OAuth Client Configuration
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9194378Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9194845Z The OAuth client you created should automatically work with any tags defined in your ACL. No additional admin console configuration is needed beyond:
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9195411Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9195636Z 1. Creating the OAuth client with appropriate scopes (`auth_keys` recommended)
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9196117Z 2. Adding the `tag:ci` tag to your ACL policy as shown above
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9196561Z 3. Adding the GitHub secrets (`TS_OAUTH_CLIENT_ID` and `TS_OAUTH_SECRET`)
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9196856Z 
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9197399Z The Tailscale GitHub Action will use the OAuth client to authenticate and automatically apply the `tag:ci` tag to the GitHub Actions runner when it connects to your tailnet.
2025-06-18T00:41:42.9198116Z </comment_old>

2025-06-18T00:44:33.5834406Z               - heading "Navigate to header Mini-history of Prefect" [level=2] [ref=e218]:
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5834871Z                 - link "Navigate to header" [ref=e220] [cursor=pointer]:
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5835236Z                   - /url: "#mini-history-of-prefect"
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5835551Z                   - img [ref=e222] [cursor=pointer]
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5835922Z                 - generic [ref=e224] [cursor=pointer]: Mini-history of Prefect
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5836289Z               - paragraph [ref=e225]:
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5836577Z                 - strong [ref=e226]: "2018-2021:"
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5838927Z                 - text: Our story begins in 2018, when we introduced the idea that workflow orchestration should be Pythonic. Inspired by distributed tools like Dask, and building on the experience of our founder, Jeremiah Lowin (a PMC member of Apache Airflow), we created a system based on simple Python decorators for tasks and flows. But what made Prefect truly special was our introduction of task mapping—a feature that would later become foundational to our dynamic execution capabilities (and eventually imitated by other orchestration SDKs).
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5841162Z               - paragraph [ref=e227]:
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5841452Z                 - strong [ref=e228]: "2022:"
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5843512Z                 - text: "Prefect’s 2.0 release became inevitable once we recognized that real-world workflows don’t always fit into neat, pre-planned DAG structures: sometimes you need to update a job definition based on runtime information, for example by skipping a branch of your workflow. So we removed a key constraint that workflows be written explicitly as DAGs, fully embracing native Python control flow—if/else conditionals, while loops-everything that makes Python…Python."
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5845269Z               - paragraph [ref=e229]:
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5845567Z                 - strong [ref=e230]: "2023-present:"
2025-06-18T00:44:33.5847027Z                 - text: With our release of Prefect 3.0 in 2024, we fully embraced these dynamic patterns by open-sourcing our events and automations backend, allowing users to natively represent event-driven workflows and gain additional observability into their execution. Prefect 3.0 also unlocked a leap forward in performance, improving the runtime overhead of Prefect by up to 90%.

You can build and schedule workflows in pure Python—no DSLs or complex config files—and run them anywhere you can run Python. Prefect handles the heavy lifting for you out of the box: automatic state tracking, failure handling, real-time monitoring, and more."

2025-06-18T00:44:41.1160075Z                 - rowgroup [ref=e60]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1160348Z                   - row [ref=e292]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1160612Z                     - cell [ref=e293]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1161118Z                       - strong [ref=e63]: Pythonic
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1162628Z                     - cell [ref=e294]: Write workflows in native Python—no DSLs, YAML, or special syntax. Full support for type hints, async/await, and modern Python patterns. Use your existing IDE, debugger, and testing tools.
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1164250Z                   - row [ref=e295]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1164719Z                     - cell [ref=e296]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1165234Z                       - strong [ref=e67]: State & Recovery
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1166853Z                     - cell [ref=e297]: Robust state management that tracks success, failure, and retry states. Resume interrupted runs from the last successful point, and cache expensive computations to avoid unnecessary rework.
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1168347Z                   - row [ref=e298]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1168842Z                     - cell [ref=e299]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1169334Z                       - strong [ref=e71]: Flexible & Portable Execution
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1171877Z                     - cell [ref=e300]: Start flows locally for easy development, then deploy them anywhere—from a single process to containers, Kubernetes, or cloud services—without locking into a vendor. Infrastructure is defined by code (not just configuration), making it simple to scale or change environments.
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1174118Z                   - row [ref=e301]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1174617Z                     - cell [ref=e302]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1174930Z                       - strong [ref=e75]: Event-Driven
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1175789Z                     - cell [ref=e303]: Trigger flows on schedules, external events, or via API. Pause flows for human intervention or approval. Chain flows together based on states, conditions, or any custom logic.
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1176603Z                   - row [ref=e304]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1176880Z                     - cell [ref=e305]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1177182Z                       - strong [ref=e79]: Dynamic Runtime
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1177954Z                     - cell [ref=e306]: Create tasks dynamically at runtime based on actual data or conditions. Easily spawn new tasks and branches during execution for truly data-driven workflows.
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1178716Z                   - row [ref=e307]:

2025-06-18T00:44:41.1180314Z                     - cell [ref=e309]: Real-time flow run monitoring, logging, and state tracking through an intuitive interface. View dependency graphs and DAGs automatically—just run your flow and open the UI.

2025-06-18T00:44:41.1224601Z               - heading [level=2] [ref=e342]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1224969Z                 - generic [ref=e224] [cursor=pointer]: Mini-history of Prefect
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1225348Z               - paragraph [ref=e225]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1225635Z                 - strong [ref=e226]: "2018-2021:"
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1228055Z                 - text: Our story begins in 2018, when we introduced the idea that workflow orchestration should be Pythonic. Inspired by distributed tools like Dask, and building on the experience of our founder, Jeremiah Lowin (a PMC member of Apache Airflow), we created a system based on simple Python decorators for tasks and flows. But what made Prefect truly special was our introduction of task mapping—a feature that would later become foundational to our dynamic execution capabilities (and eventually imitated by other orchestration SDKs).
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1230070Z               - paragraph [ref=e227]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1230363Z                 - strong [ref=e228]: "2022:"
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1232521Z                 - text: "Prefect’s 2.0 release became inevitable once we recognized that real-world workflows don’t always fit into neat, pre-planned DAG structures: sometimes you need to update a job definition based on runtime information, for example by skipping a branch of your workflow. So we removed a key constraint that workflows be written explicitly as DAGs, fully embracing native Python control flow—if/else conditionals, while loops-everything that makes Python…Python."
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1234304Z               - paragraph [ref=e229]:
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1234597Z                 - strong [ref=e230]: "2023-present:"
2025-06-18T00:44:41.1236077Z                 - text: With our release of Prefect 3.0 in 2024, we fully embraced these dynamic patterns by open-sourcing our events and automations backend, allowing users to natively represent event-driven workflows and gain additional observability into their execution. Prefect 3.0 also unlocked a leap forward in performance, improving the runtime overhead of Prefect by up to 90%.

2025-06-18T00:44:48.5223008Z                     - cell [ref=e297]: Robust state management that tracks success, failure, and retry states. Resume interrupted runs from the last successful point, and cache expensive computations to avoid unnecessary rework.

2025-06-18T00:44:48.5225866Z                     - cell [ref=e300]: Start flows locally for easy development, then deploy them anywhere—from a single process to containers, Kubernetes, or cloud services—without locking into a vendor. Infrastructure is defined by code (not just configuration), making it simple to scale or change environments.

2025-06-18T00:44:48.5324333Z                 - generic [ref=e224] [cursor=pointer]: Mini-history of Prefect
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5324987Z               - paragraph [ref=e225]:
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5325473Z                 - strong [ref=e226]: "2018-2021:"
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5329774Z                 - text: Our story begins in 2018, when we introduced the idea that workflow orchestration should be Pythonic. Inspired by distributed tools like Dask, and building on the experience of our founder, Jeremiah Lowin (a PMC member of Apache Airflow), we created a system based on simple Python decorators for tasks and flows. But what made Prefect truly special was our introduction of task mapping—a feature that would later become foundational to our dynamic execution capabilities (and eventually imitated by other orchestration SDKs).
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5333607Z               - paragraph [ref=e227]:
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5334101Z                 - strong [ref=e228]: "2022:"
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5337959Z                 - text: "Prefect’s 2.0 release became inevitable once we recognized that real-world workflows don’t always fit into neat, pre-planned DAG structures: sometimes you need to update a job definition based on runtime information, for example by skipping a branch of your workflow. So we removed a key constraint that workflows be written explicitly as DAGs, fully embracing native Python control flow—if/else conditionals, while loops-everything that makes Python…Python."
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5341345Z               - paragraph [ref=e229]:
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5341847Z                 - strong [ref=e230]: "2023-present:"
2025-06-18T00:44:48.5344525Z                 - text: With our release of Prefect 3.0 in 2024, we fully embraced these dynamic patterns by open-sourcing our events and automations backend, allowing users to natively represent event-driven workflows and gain additional observability into their execution. Prefect 3.0 also unlocked a leap forward in performance, improving the runtime overhead of Prefect by up to 90%.
